= Monash Japanese Program

[.right]
[link=https://research.monash.edu/en/persons/iori-hamada/]
image::https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/05/profile.png[profile, width=240]

// https://open.umn.edu/opentextbooks/subjects/languages
https://open.umn.edu/opentextbooks/textbooks/1941[Japanese Introductory 1] 是明尼苏达大学 (University of Minnesota) 开放教科书图书馆 (Open Textbook Library, OTL) 收录的一本开源的日语入门教材，作者是濱田伊織 (Iori Hamada) 博士，来自澳大利亚维多利亚州公立高校——蒙纳士大学（Monash University）。上智大学文学部教育学科卒業。2006年、オーストラリア政府エンデバー奨学金受賞者として、王立メルボルン工科大学修士課程でコミュニケーション学を専攻、2007年、同大学で修士号取得、成績優秀者として卒業。

此书目标是为读者教授日语入门知识，难度定位在欧洲共同语言参考标准 (Common European Framework of Reference for Languages, CEFR) A1 级 (入门级) ，内容包括：掌握全部平假名、片假名，约50-100个基础汉字；最基础的语法框架（如：~です、~ます、~てください等），约30-50个句型；能看懂简单的自我介绍、贺卡、菜单等50-100字的短文。本文内容在源共享文件的基础上做了汉字增补和说明，后续还考虑增补 JLPT N3 到 N1 级别的知识点，暂且称之为《日语入门增补版》。源文档中使用 H5P 制作的互动测试转换为相应的 HTML 内容，答案可以通过鼠标悬停在空缺位或者备选答案末尾即会显示，方便在线阅读和自测。此外，在正式自学日语的过程中，结合认知科学探索并总结了多语言学习的方法 (link:./LaTeX/linguistics.adoc[多语言学习文档])，并应用学习实践过程中，发现应试教育给不到的那种满足探索欲望的快乐。这让我狠狠地共鸣起小时候自学自行车时的快乐，通过微调身体姿态并稳定地立身于自行车平衡点中，让身体与自行车融为一体，沿着斜坡飞驰的感觉毕生难忘。

日本语能力测试（JLPT／The Japanese-Language Proficiency Test）是日本国际交流基金会主办的，面向母语为非日语人士的语言能力测试。最高为 N1 级别，相当于我国大学本科专业日语 3～4 年级的水平，也是赴日留学或商务等活动的基本要求。N1、N2 考试分“语言知识（文字、词汇、语法）、阅读”和“听力” 2 个科目，N3、N4、N5 考试分“语言知识（文字、词汇）”；“语言知识（语法）、阅读”及“听力”3个科目，满分均为 180 分。N1、N2 级考试费为 550 元人民币每人每次。成绩通知书由「日本国际交流基金会」通过教育部考试中心及下设考点发放给考生本人，成绩合格者还将同时得到日方颁发的合格证书。通过「综合得分」和各「单项得分」的及格分来评定是否及格，因此各单项成绩需要达到合格分数才可能获得合格证书。国内考试报名可登录中国教育考试网 https://jlpt.neea.cn/[公网] 或  https://jlpt.neea.edu.cn/[教育网]。

这个系列教材包括 Japanese Introductory 1/2, Intermediate 1/2, Proficient 1/2，采用的是 Monash University (莫纳什大学) 日语课程的分级体系，这套教材是该校依据《大家的日语》或《GENKI》等主流教材内容编排。教材内容编排参考 https://www.monash.edu/arts/languages-literatures-cultures-linguistics/japanese-program-content[Japanese Program Content]。

•  <<jpc,   Japanese Program Content>>
•  <<japi1, Japanese Introductory 1 Content>>
•  <<japi2, Japanese Introductory 2 Content>>
•  <<japm1, Japanese Intermediate 1 Content>>
•  <<japm2, Japanese Intermediate 2 Content>>
•  <<japp,  Japanese Proficient 1 and 2 Content>>

GENKI（『初級日本語げんき』） 是目前全球最主流的日语综合教材之一，全系列共 2 册，总计 23 课，适用于 JLPT N5/N4 水平，目前更新出版到第三版。2009 年出版的 Tobira: Gateway to Advanced Japanese 最初是经典的中级到高级过渡教材 (JLPT N3 → N2/N1)，但在 2021-2022 年，该系列增加初级部分 (JLPT N5/N4)，扩展成了覆盖完整三段式的体系，此前在 2009 年出版的经典教材由原作者团队全面修订，并拆分为全新的两册教材：TOBIRA 系列 = 初级（全2册） + 中级（全2册，原版拆分而来）。

•  Tobira: Gateway to Advanced Japanese (2009)
•  Tobira I: Beginning Japanese (2021)
•  Tobira II: Beginning Japanese (2022)
•  TOBIRA I: Intermediate Japanese 包含原版教材第 1-8 课，已于 2025 年底出版。
•  TOBIRA II: Intermediate Japanese 包含剩余内容（第9-15课），预计于 2026 年夏季出版。

CEFR 分三个层次（Basic/Indepentent/Profcient）七个级别：C2 (Profcient - 熟练) 对应 IELTS 7.5 - 8.0；C1 (Advanced) 对应 IELTS 6.5；B2 (Independent - 独立运用, First for Schools) 对应 IELTS 5.0 - 6.0；B1 (Preliminary for Schools) 对应 IELTS 4.5；A2 (Flyers) 对应 IELTS 4 分以下；A1 Movers, Key for Schools；Pre A1 Starters。

•  https://pdxscholar.library.pdx.edu/pdxopen/6/[Beginning Japanese for Professionals: Book 1 by Konomi, Emiko, 2015] https://open.umn.edu/opentextbooks/textbooks/beginning-japanese-for-professionals-book-1[on OTL]
•  https://pdxscholar.library.pdx.edu/pdxopen/17/[Beginning Japanese for Professionals: Book 2 by Konomi, Emiko, 2017] https://open.umn.edu/opentextbooks/textbooks/beginning-japanese-for-professionals-book-2[on OTL]
•  https://pdxscholar.library.pdx.edu/pdxopen/22/[Beginning Japanese for Professionals: Book 3 by Konomi, Emiko, 2018] https://open.umn.edu/opentextbooks/textbooks/beginning-japanese-for-professionals-book-3[on OTL]

NOTE: Beginning Japanese for Professionals 这套开源书籍同样是入门级别教材 (JLPT N5/N4)，来自波特兰州立大学，专门给商学院国际管理硕士项目学生编写的专业教材。目标就是跨国工作，所以学的日语是针对真实职场专业场景对话。与传统教材谈校园生活不同，这本书的对话围绕着 初到公司、项目组开会、交换名片、通勤交通、购物、日程 等十个话题展开。配音录制基本按照对话为单位录制为一个 Wav 音频文件，对话脚本内容或涉及的词汇会在每章后尾的 Drill Tape Script 中展示。作者 Emiko Konomi 博士不仅是语言学专家，更常年在商学院任教，还获得过杰出教学奖。更值得一提的是，她在美国训练过多名日语老师。Emiko 在教学中的特色也与一般教材不同：直接向学生提供大量优质的具有示范作用句子，并融合文法内容和词汇运用。配合优质的音频做听觉输入的训练、配合文字做视觉输入训练、配合角色扮演做语言输出能力的训练，这是一套正真符合认知科学、母语学习法的入门教材。音频提供了足够留白时间，学习者可以跟读，或者默读，也可以跟音频进行模拟对话。可惜的是，尽管这套教材给出了五十音图和日语音节，以拉丁字母做音标，假定学习者掌握的日语假名和发音系统，没有掌握日语发音的学习者需要先行学习这个前置基础。应试教材普遍有“内容填塞有余、认知系统缺陷”的毛病，对于有能力消化的学习者来说是可以的，但是对于缺失认知科学方法方面知识、又消化不了巨大内容量的学生来说，这种教材只会压垮他们的学习态度。如果教材能融合 Emiko 这样的母语学习法和内容量大的优点，那将是非常一级棒的。或者说，学习者需要主动去融合市面上的教材，来实现这样棒的自定学习系统。

image::https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/12/OER-coversJAPANESE8--677x1024.jpg[cover]
[.text-center]
**Japanese Introductory 1 by Iori Hamada**

Book Description: Japanese Introductory 1 is a comprehensive and engaging textbook for beginners eager to learn Japanese. Tailored to fit the needs of self-learners and students in academic settings alike, this book aligns with the semester timelines of universities in Australia and New Zealand. The book offers interactive exercises, cultural insights and a diverse perspective, including Indigenous voices from both Australia and Japan. It begins with familiar contexts, gradually exploring broader aspects of Japanese culture and language. Structured to resonate with learners from various backgrounds, it is a practical and engaging resource for those starting their journey into the Japanese language.

Japanese Introductory 1 Copyright © 2024 by Iori Hamada is licensed under a Creative Commons Attribution-NonCommercial 4.0 International License, except where otherwise noted.

== Acknowledgements

I would like to express my profound gratitude to all those who have contributed to bringing this project to fruition. First and foremost, I am deeply indebted to the exceptional Monash librarian team – Susie Phillips, Mitchell Reid, Jenny Fafeita, Mia Goodwin, and former librarian Veronica Delafosse – whose expertise and dedication were instrumental throughout this journey. Special thanks to Copyright Adviser Megan Deacon for her invaluable guidance.

I extend my sincere appreciation to the two anonymous reviewers whose thoughtful and critical feedback significantly enhanced the quality of this work. I am also grateful to all my students who provided valuable feedback on early drafts, helping to shape and refine this work.

This manuscript has also benefited immensely from the meticulous attention of Dr Sara Kitaoji, who undertook the considerable task of proofreading over 100,000 words across all chapters.

Additionally, I have been fortunate to receive generous support from the academic community. I would like to extend my special thanks to Dr Thomas Budinette and Dr Wes Robertson from Macquarie University for their invaluable feedback on the textbook, particularly their insights on gender perspectives, which greatly enriched the material on Japanese pronouns and gender.

I am especially grateful to Associate Professors Beatrice Trefalt and Jeremy Breaden from the Japanese Studies Program for their unwavering support. Their belief in this ambitious project, assistance with the Council of Australian University Librarians grant application, and consistent encouragement were instrumental to its realisation. This project simply would not have been possible without their support.

[[intro]]

== Introduction

=== The Features

1. SLIDE 1: What is Japanese Introductory 1?
+
[cols="2,1"]
|===
a|
This open textbook is interactive, authentic and accessible.

It is an easy-to-use, engaging and practical guide to learning Japanese language and culture. 
a|
image::{CURI}46/images/file-64b31b3ce043f.jpg[figure,width=320]
|===

2. SLIDE 2: Who is it for?
+
[cols="2,1"]
|===
a|
This textbook is crafted with **beginners** in mind.

If you have never stepped foot in a Japanese class, or if you have only had a small taste of the language, this resource is **for you**!
a|
image::{CURI}46/images/file-64d1555a1ccaa.jpg[figure,width=320]
|===

3. SLIDE 3: Who is it for?
+
[cols="2,1"]
|===
a|
The textbook has also been tailored to meet the academic requirements of **universities in Australia and New Zealand**. 

It fits smoothly into **a 12-week semester timetable**.
a|
image::{CURI}46/images/file-64d155b903ad3.jpg[figure,width=320]
|===

4. SLIDE 4: Who is it for?
+
[cols="2,1"]
|===
a|
But that is not all.

If you are looking to journey into the Japanese language on your own, this textbook has got you covered.

It is filled with interactive review exercises designed with **the independent learner** in mind.
a|
image::{CURI}46/images/file-64d156f819711.jpg[figure,width=320]
|===

5. SLIDE 5: Who is it for?
+
[cols="2,1"]
|===
a|
So, whether you are a self-study enthusiast or a university student, this textbook is **for you**!

It is designed to be a **friendly guide** on your path to mastering the Japanese language.
a|
image::{CURI}46/images/file-64d15811daaf9.jpg[figure,width=320]
|===

6. SLIDE 6: What is special about it?
+
[cols="2,1"]
|===
a|
Well, it starts with what is **familiar** to you! 

It understands that many learners are not in Japan.

The textbook initiates the learning process with situations that you already know and relate to.

From there, it **gently expands your horizons**, guiding you to understand Japanese culture and language.
a|
image::{CURI}46/images/file-64d159948bb18.jpg[figure,width=320]
|===

7. SLIDE 7: What is special about it?
+
[cols="2,1"]
|===
a|
Moreover, the textbook makes an effort to remain close to **real-life scenarios**.

It places a strong emphasis on **practical, day-to-day situations**.

This means that the skills you pick up are not just for textbooks, but **can be used in the real world**.
a|
image::{CURI}46/images/file-64b315c295451.jpg[figure,width=320]
|===

8. SLIDE 8: What is special about it?
+
[cols="2,1"]
|===
a|
Last but not least, the textbook shows its dedication to creating a **diverse and inclusive space for learning**.

It goes a step further by including insights from **indigenous communities in both Australia and Japan**.

This means that while you're learning the language, you're also getting a fresh take on the culture that goes **beyond the Euro-American centric view**. 
a|
image::{CURI}46/images/file-64d15a7e7e45c.jpg[figure,width=320]
|===


=== The User Guide

1. Slide 1: What is the book structure?
** Chapter 1.  Head Start with Japanese
** Chapter 2.  Welcome to Japanese Learning Communities (1)
** Chapter 3.  Welcome to Japanese Learning Communities (2)
** Chapter 4. Bringing Japan Home (1)
** Chapter 5. Bringing Japan Home (2)
** Chapter 6. Expanding Your Japnaese Toolkit (1)
** Chapter 7. Expanding Your Japnaese Toolkit (2)
** Chapter 8. Getting Ready to Take on Japan (1)
** Chapter 9. Getting Ready to Take on Japan (2)
** Chapter 10. Getting Around Japan (1)
** Chapter 11. Getting Around Japan (2)
** Chapter 12. Looking Back, Moving Forward

2. Slide 2: Where to start?
+
[cols="2,1"]
|===
a|
The journey begins with **Chapter 1: Head Start with Hiragana**.

This chapter is designed to help you build confidence with the **47 hiragana characters**, making them feel familiar and approachable.

If you're studying within a structured curriculum, this chapter serves as an **ideal pre-semester primer**, ensuring you're well-prepared to hit the ground running when classes begin.

a|
image::{CURI}47/images/file-64d15ff55f6eb.jpg[figure,width=320]
|===

3. Slide 3: How does this fit into a semester's curriculum?
+
[cols="2,1"]
|===
a|
For students in Australia and New Zealand, the remaining chapters are spread evenly across **the 12-week semester**.

This means you can focus on mastering **one chapter each week**.
a|
image::{CURI}47/images/file-64d16133d03ea.jpg[figure,width=320]
|===

4. Slide 4: What is in each chapter?
+
[cols="2,1"]
|===
a|
Now, what can you expect in each chapter?

Every chapter opens with a multi-media opening page featuring:

• **Key words and expressions** to focus on.
• **A model dialogue** to help you see the language in action.
• **Further resources on cultural aspects**, giving you more than just language learning.
a|
image::{CURI}47/images/file-64d1735ad2fa4.jpg[figure,width=320]
|===

5. Slide 5: How does each chapter work?
+
[cols="2,1"]
|===
a|
Following the opening page, each chapter is divided into **a few modules**.

Each module is designed to offer a learning experience that lasts between **10 to 15 minutes**.

These modules delve into essential aspects of Japanese language and culture, **covering the writing and sound systems, grammar rules and cultural insights**.

It is like your **personal toolbox**, helping you navigate the language with ease.
a|
image::{CURI}47/images/file-64d174442d148.jpg[figure,width=320]
|===

6. Slide 6: How does each chapter work?
+
[cols="2,1"]
|===
a|
At the end of each chapter, you will find a **'Review'** section waiting for you.

This section is designed to recap all that you have learned and accomplished.

It even includes **a summative review exercise** to help you reinforce and fully embrace the concepts you have explored in the chapter!
a|
image::{CURI}47/images/file-64da95999db29.jpg[figure,width=320]
|===

7. Slide 7: Any other information that I should know about the textbook?
+
[cols="2,1"]
|===
a|
All the Japanese texts - hiragana, katakana and kanji - are **romanised**.

We've designed it this way to **help you master hiragana first**.

But don't worry, words written in katakana are also accompanied by their hiragana reading!
a|
image::{CURI}47/images/file-64d178f57d23b.jpg[figure,width=320]
|===

8. Slide 8: Any other information that I should know about the textbook?
+
[cols="2,1"]
|===
a|
Our textbook doesn't just stick to formal speech styles.

We also dive into **the fascinating differences between formal and informal ways of speaking in Japanese**.

By exploring both, we aim to help learners grasp when to use each style.

It's all part of making sure you feel confident and at ease in various social settings!
a|
image::{CURI}47/images/file-64d8745cdb770.jpg[figure,width=320]
|===

9. Slide 9: Any other information that I should know about the textbook?
+
[cols="2,1"]
|===
a|
You will learn **kanji characters** starting from Chapter 7, which is about halfway through the textbook.

We hope this makes your learning journey even smoother.

Happy learning!
a|
image::{CURI}47/images/file-64d179e0471c4.jpg[figure,width=320]
|===

[[author]]
== About the Author

Dr Iori Hamada is a Lecturer in Japanese Studies at Monash University, Australia. Born and raised in Japan, Iori relocated to Australia in 2006 as a recipient of the Endeavour Japan Award conferred by the Australian Government. In 2019, she was awarded https://academic.oup.com/ssjj/pages/prize_winning_articles?login=false[the Institute of Social Science and Oxford University Press Prize] for her co-authored journal article titled ‘https://academic.oup.com/ssjj/article/22/1/109/5273642[Silent Exits: Risk and Post-3.11 Skilled Migration from Japan to Australia]’. In 2021, Iori was also awarded a https://www.advance-he.ac.uk/fellowship/fellowship[Fellowship of Higher Education Academy (FHEA) by Advance Higher Education&#44; UK].

Iori’s research is centred on interdisciplinary approaches aimed at fostering social inclusion. Her work spans various fields, such as labour migration, gender equality and food production and consumption, all interconnected by the goal of creating a more inclusive and equitable society.

She has penned several language learning books that have been translated into Chinese and Korean.

In addition to her language learning books, Iori is the author of https://www.routledge.com/The-Japanese-Restaurant-Tasting-the-New-Exotic-in-Australia/Hamada/p/book/9781032423685[The Japanese Restaurant: Tasting the New Exotic in Australia] (Routledge, 2024). The book has been honoured with the 31st Gourmand Award. She served as lead editor, together with Professors Yoshikazu Shiobara (Keio University) and Takeshi Hamano (The University of Kitakyushu), for the edited volume titled ‘https://www.routledge.com/Japanese-Migrations-to-Australia-Transformation-and-Heterogeneity/Hamada-Hamano-Shiobara/p/book/9781032950105[Japanese Migrations to Australia: Transformation and Heterogeneity]‘ (Routledge, 2025).

Iori can be contacted via iori.hamada@monash.edu.

This resource was created with reference to the https://www.w3.org/WAI/standards-guidelines/wcag/[WCAG guidelines] and aims to meet the criteria listed on our https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/36/2023/09/Accessibility-checklist.pdf[Accessibility Checklist.]

If you have problems accessing this resource, please fill in the https://forms.gle/vWZSfS5wDZxM8FbP6[Monash open textbook feedback form.]

[[japi1]]
== Japanese Introductory 1 Content

=== Grammar

Japanese Introductory 1 Unit Codes: http://www.monash.edu.au/pubs/handbooks/units/ATS1141.html[ATS1141], http://www.monash.edu.au/pubs/handbooks/units/ATS2141.html[ATS2141]

*Textbook: GENKI I* AN INTEGRATED COURSE IN ELEMENTARY JAPANESE *[LESSON 1-6]*

*Supplementary Online Textbook:* *https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/japanese[Japanese Introductory 1]*

[width="100%",cols="50%,50%",]
|===
|*Grammar Points* |*Example Sentences*
a|
* XはYです
* XはYじゃないです
* Nouns (present/past)
* Adjectives (present/past)

|メアリーさんはアメリカ人(じん)です。 +
 メアリーさんはアメリカ人じゃないです。 +
 大学生(だいがくせい)です/大学生でした +
 たかいです/たかかったです
a|
* Question sentences

|メアリーさんはアメリカ人ですか。
a|
* Numbers (1~10万)
* 時間(じかん)
* でんわばんごう

|何時(なんじ)におきますか。 +
 八時(はちじ)におきます。 +
 でんわばんごうは356-6520です。
a|
* これ/それ/あれ/どれ

|これは何(なん)ですか。
a|
* だれの + Noun

|これはだれの本(ほん)ですか。
a|
* Particles (の/も/を/で/に/へ/は/と/ね/よ)
* Verbs (present/past)
* Adverbs of frequency

|食(た)べます/飲(の)みます +
 食べました/飲みました +
 毎日(まいにち)、コーヒーを飲みます。
a|
* ～ませんか
* ～ましょう

|テレビを見(み)ませんか。 +
 テレビを見ましょう。
a|
* Xがあります
* Xがいます

|ぎんこうがあります。 +
ねこがいます。
a|
* XはYの前(まえ)/上(うえ)/中(なか)です

|学校(がっこう)はぎんこうの前(まえ)です。
a|
* Verbs _te_ form
* ～てください
* ～てもいいです
* ～てはいけません
* Connecting sentences by _te_ form

a|食べて/飲んで +
 ドアをあけてください。 +
 テレビを見てもいいですか。 +
 あさごはんを食べて、大学に行(い)きます。

a|
* (Reason) から

|べんきょうします。あしたテストがありますから。
|===

_Source: GENKI I_

=== Vocabulary

*Approximately 350 words* will be introduced in Japanese Introductory 1.

Main types of vocabularies are related to:

* country names
* occupation names
* names of family members
* food names
* place names
* school life
* expressions of time/age/numbers

See an https://www.monash.edu/arts/languages-literatures-cultures-linguistics/japanese-program-content/japanese-program-vocabulary[overview of vocabulary] across all units.

=== Kanji

.Approximately 60 kanji will be introduced in Japanese Introductory 1.
[%autowdth, cols="1",role="big"]
|===
| *第 3 课 (1-15)* 
| 一（いち）二（に）三（さん）四（よん）五（ご）六（ろく）七（しち）八（はち）九（きょう）十（じゅう）百（ひゃく）千（せん）万（まん）円（えん）時（じ）
| *第 4 课 (16-29)* 
| 日（にち）本（ぼん）人（じん）月（げつ）火（か）水（すい）木（もく）金（きん）土（ど） 
| *第 5 课 (30-43)* 
| 山（やま）川（かわ）元（げん）気（き）天（てん）私（し）今（こん）田（た）女（おんあ）男（たん）見（み）行（いく）食（し）飲（のみ）
| *第 6 课 (44-58)* 
| 東（とう・ひがし）西（さい・にし）南（なん・みなみ）北（ほく・きた）口（くち）出（で）右（みぎ）左（ひだり）分（わ・ふん）先（さき）生（せい・なま）大（だい・おお）学（がく・まなぶ）外（がい・そと・はずす）国（こく・くに）
|===

NOTE: 「東西線」读作 とうざいせん（连浊），不是「とうさいせん」。「東＋西」特殊浊化，是因为它作为一个固定线路名称长期固化读法。这条线连接中野（西侧） 和西船桥（东侧），因此叫“东西线”。

[[japi2]]
== Japanese Introductory 2 Content

=== Grammar

Japanese Introductory 2 Unit Codes: http://www.monash.edu.au/pubs/handbooks/units/ATS1142.html[ATS1142], http://www.monash.edu.au/pubs/handbooks/units/ATS2142.html[ATS2142]

*Textbook: GENKI I* AN INTEGRATED COURSE IN ELEMENTARY JAPANESE *[LESSON 7-12]*

[width="100%",cols="50%a,50%a",]
|===
|*Grammar Points* |*Example Sentences*
|
* XはYがZです
|メアリーさんはかみが長(なが)いです。
|
* Adjective/Noun _te_ form
|大学(だいがく)はあたらしくて、きれいです。
|
* ～ている
|テレビを見(み)ています。 +
けっこんしています。
|
* Verb + に行(い)く
|えいがを見に行(い)きます。
|
* Verbs _nai_ form
* ～ないでください
* ～なければいけません/～なきゃいけません
|食(た)べない/飲(の)まない +
 見ないでください。 +
 六時(ろくじ)におきなければいけません。 +
 六時におきなきゃいけません。
|
* Short form
|学生(がくせい)だ/高(たか)くない/行く +
 学生だった/高くなかった/行った
|
* Particles (が/で/や)
* Adjective/Noun + の
|大(おお)きいのをください。
|
* まだ + Negative
|ひるごはんをまだ食べていません。
|
* (Reason) から、…
* (Reason) ので、…
|テストがあるから、べんきょうします。 +
テストなので、べんきょうします。
|
* Counters: Person
* Counters: Ordinal numbers
|学生が五人(ごにん)います。 +
 ケーキを三つ(みっつ)ください。
|
* Comparison among two
* Comparison among more than three
|電車(でんしゃ)のほうがバスよりはやいです。 +
 ひこうきがいちばんはやいです。
|
* Adjective + なる
|きれいになりました。
|
* ～つもり
|明日(あした)、友(とも)だちにあうつもりです。
|
* ～たい
|ハンバーガーを食べたいです。
|
* ～たり、～たり
* ～たことがある
|テレビを見たり、本(ほん)を読(よ)んだりします。 +
 日本(にほん)に行ったことがありますか。
|
* ～すぎる
|食べすぎました。
|
* ～ほうがいい
|病院(びょういん)へ行ったほうがいいです。
|
* ～んです
|どうしたんですか。
|
* ～でしょう
|明日は雨(あめ)でしょう。
|===

_Source: GENKI I_


=== Vocabulary

*Approximately 350 words* will be introduced in Japanese Introductory 2.

Main types of vocabularies are related to:

* names of body parts
* colour names
* description of health and illness

See an https://www.monash.edu/arts/languages-literatures-cultures-linguistics/japanese-program-content/japanese-program-vocabulary[overview of vocabulary] across all units.

[[japm1]]
== Japanese Intermediate 1 Content

=== Grammar

Japanese Intermediate 1 Unit Codes: http://www.monash.edu.au/pubs/handbooks/units/ATS2143.html[ATS2143]

*Textbook: GENKI II* AN INTEGRATED COURSE IN ELEMENTARY JAPANESE *[LESSON 13-18]*

[width="100%",cols="50%a,50%a",]
|===
|*Grammar Points* |*Example Sentences*
|
* Counters (こ/さつ/ひき/ほん/だい/まい)
| 
|
* Potential form
* Volitional form
|日本語が話せます。 +
 日本に行こうと思います。
|
* ～し
|あのレストランはおいしいし、やすいし、また行きたいです。
|
* ～そう (look like)
* ～そう (I heard)
* ～みたい
|このケーキはおしいそうです。 +
 このケーキはおいしいそうです。 +
 あの人はモデルみたいです。
|
* ～てみる
* ～ておく
* ～てしまう
|その映画を見てみます。 +
 チケットを買っておきます。 +
 ぜんぶ食べてしまいました。
|
* ～たらどう
* ～たら
|早くねたらどうですか。 +
 天気がよかったら、ピクニックに行きます。
|
* あげる/くれる/もらう
* ～てあげる/～てくれる/～てもらう
|私は友だちにプレゼントをあげました。 +
 私は母に朝ごはんを作ってあげました。
|
* ～なくてもいい
|明日は、大学に行かなくてもいいです。
|
* ～かもしれない
|明日は雨がふるかもしれません。
|
* Qualifier sentences
|これは父がくれた本です。
|
* Number + も
* Number + しか + Negative
|十人もいます。 +
 十人しかいません。
|
* ～時
|大学に行く時、バスにのります。
|
* ～前に
* ～てから
|日本に行く前に、日本語を勉強します。 +
 日本に行ってから、日本語を勉強します。
|
* Transitive/Intransitive verbs
|あける/あく
|
* ～と
|はるになると、あたたかくなります。
|
* ～ながら
|おんがくを聞きながら勉強します。
|===

_Source: GENKI II_

=== Vocabulary

*Approximately 350 words* will be introduced in Japanese Intermediate 1.

See an https://www.monash.edu/arts/languages-literatures-cultures-linguistics/japanese-program-content/japanese-program-vocabulary[overview of vocabulary] across all units.

[[japm2]]
== Japanese Intermediate 2 Content

=== Grammar

Japanese Intermediate 2 Unit Codes: http://www.monash.edu.au/pubs/handbooks/units/ATS2144.html[ATS2144] **

*Textbook: GENKI II* AN INTEGRATED COURSE IN ELEMENTARY JAPANESE *[LESSON 19-23]*

[width="100%",cols="50%,50%",]
|===
|*Grammar Points* |*Example Sentences*
|
* Honorific verbs
* Humble verbs
* Extra-modest expressions
|お書きになります/くださいます +
 お書きします/さしあげます +
 まいります
|
* ～はず
|今日はテストがあるはずです。
|
* ～ないで
|朝ごはんを食べないで、学校に行きました。
|
* Question within larger sentences
|テストはいつか知っていますか。 +
 あの人が学生かどうかわかりません。
|
* ～やすい
* ～にくい
|この町はすみやすいです。 +
 この町はすみにくいです。
|
* Passive sentences
* Causative sentences
* Causative-passive sentences
|私は弟にケーキを食べられました。 +
 母は私にそうじをさせました。 +
 私は母にそうじをさせられました。
|
* ～てある
* ～てほしい
* ～ても
|ドアが開けてあります。 +
 友だちにしゅくだいをてつだってほしいです。 +
 雨がふっても、ピクニックに行きます。
|
* ～あいだに
|出かけているあいだに、友だちが来ました。
|
* Adjective + する
|へやをきれいにしました。
|
* Verb + なさい
|早く学校へ行きなさい。
|
* ～ば
|このくすりを飲めば、だいじょうぶです。
|
* ～のに
|早く家を出たのに、クラスにおくれました。
|
* ～のような
* ～のように
|メルボルンのような町がすきです。 +
 さかなのようにおよげます。
|
* ～ことにする
|来年、日本に行くことにしました。
|
* ～まで
|テストがおわるまであそべません。
|
* ～方
|このかんじの読み方をおしえてください。
|===

_Source: GENKI II_


=== Vocabulary

*Approximately 300 words* will be introduced in Japanese Intermediate 2.

See an https://www.monash.edu/arts/languages-literatures-cultures-linguistics/japanese-program-content/japanese-program-vocabulary[overview of vocabulary] across all units.

=== Kanji

.Approximately 80 kanji will be introduced in Japanese Intermediate 2.
[cols="1",role="big"]
|===

|oo:春[•　しゅん / はる　•] 
 oo:秋[•　しゅう / あき　•] 
 oo:冬[•　とう / ふゆ　•] 
 oo:花[•　か / はな　•] 
 oo:樸[•　ぼく / あら(き)　•] 
 oo:不[•　ふ / ぶ / ず　•] 
 oo:姉[•　し / あね　•] 
 oo:兄[•　けい / あに　•] 
 oo:漢[•　かん　•] 
 oo:卒[•　そつ　•] 
 oo:工[•　こう / く　•]

[[japp]]
== Japanese Proficient 1 and 2 Content

Japanese Proficient 1 Unit Codes: http://www.monash.edu.au/pubs/handbooks/units/ATS2145.html[ATS2145], http://www.monash.edu.au/pubs/handbooks/units/ATS3145.html[ATS3145]

Japanese Proficient 2 Unit Codes: http://www.monash.edu.au/pubs/handbooks/units/ATS2146.html[ATS2146], http://www.monash.edu.au/pubs/handbooks/units/ATS3146.html[ATS3146]

*Textbooks* ::
1. _TOBIRA: Gateway to Advanced Japanese Learning through content and Multimedia_ +
[Proficient 1: Chapters 1-4, Proficient 2: Chapters 5-8] +
2. _TOBIRA: Power Up Your KANJI: 800 Basic KANJI as a Gateway to Advanced Japanese_ +
[Proficient 1: Chapters 1-4, Proficient 2: Chapters 5-8]

*Prior to these units* ::
•  Kanji level: 300 characters
•  Vocabulary: 1100 words
•  Grammar: refer to https://www.monash.edu/arts/languages-literatures-cultures-linguistics/japanese-program-content/japanese-introductory-1-content[Introductory 1] & https://www.monash.edu/arts/languages-literatures-cultures-linguistics/japanese-program-content/japanese-introductory-2-content[2] and https://www.monash.edu/arts/languages-literatures-cultures-linguistics/japanese-program-content/japanese-intermediate-1-content[Intermediate 1] & https://www.monash.edu/arts/languages-literatures-cultures-linguistics/japanese-program-content/japanese-intermediate-2-content[2] content overviews

*Differences from Intermediate 1 & 2* ::
•  Japanese Proficient 1 and 2 are not organized based on the accumulation of grammar points.
•  Proficient 1 & 2 builds on the grammar taught in Introductory 1 & 2 and Intermediate 1 & 2, introducing more advanced grammar at the same time. Importantly, the focus is on application of grammar in conjunction with pragmatic expressions and more academic contexts. Students are expected to expand reading, writing, speaking, and listening skills, applying what they have developed.
•  Proficient 1 & 2 are organized topically.

*Cultural topics* ::
•  Proficient 1: Geography, martial arts and sports, technology, etc.
•  Proficient 2: Popular culture, traditional culture, religion in Japan, etc.

*Research skills* ::
•  Searching information in Japanese and using online tools to read authentic texts in Japanese
•  Basic skills in academic writing in Japanese: citation, references
•  Interviewing in Japanese

*Grammar* ::
•  Solidifying the basic grammar built during students’ previous study (refer to Introductory and Intermediate unit content
•  Knowledge of complex sentence structure to analyse and understand reading texts
•  Conjunctions to produce coherent sentences and paragraphs

*Reception – Listening and reading* ::
•  Comprehending long texts (1,200-2000 characters) in each lesson
•  Reading and listening to semi-authentic and authentic texts – articles, dialogues, autobiographies, _manga_, myths, etc.
•  Listening for picking up various types of information including general ideas, key points, etc.
•  Understanding lectures in Japanese
•  Strategies – using schema and guessing, speed reading and extensive reading, using a dictionary, online tools, analysing texts

*Spoken interaction and production including sociolinguistic knowledge, discourse, and communication strategies* ::
•  Ability to manage speech levels properly: politeness, gendered language, written and spoken language, interpersonal relations and language
•  Communication strategies to sustain a conversation: asking for clarification, _aizuchi_, fillers, responding quickly, etc.
•  Colloquial language to understand popular culture such as onomatopoeia
•  Stating an opinion, giving an explanation, telling a story, managing a presentation, etc.

*Written interaction and production* ::
•  Writing connecting sentences and paragraphs
•  Choosing an appropriate style of writing
•  Writing an email in Japanese
•  Writing a 800-1,000 character essay unassisted in an exam setting, and a 1600-2000 character essay assisted with dictionaries and references
•  Kanji: Approx 300 new kanji to be introduced (150 each in Proficient 1 and 2; Total – 600 kanji


[[vocab]]
=== Vocabulary

*Approximately 350 words* will be introduced in Japanese Proficient 1.

*Approximately 350 words* will be introduced in Japanese Proficient 2.

See an https://www.monash.edu/arts/languages-literatures-cultures-linguistics/japanese-program-content/japanese-program-vocabulary[overview of vocabulary] across all units.

= Chapter 1: Head Start with Japanese

[[c01_0]]
== 1.0 Head Start with Japanese

.Sakura Landscape
[.text-center]
[link=https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/pexels-ryutaro-tsukata-5220046.jpg]
image::https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/pexels-ryutaro-tsukata-5220046-1024x683.jpg[Cheery Blossom,width=904,height=602]

If you are looking for a language that brings excitement and practicality, Japanese is an excellent choice. With its rich culture and influence on entertainment, technology and business, Japanese is a language that can unlock many opportunities and enrich your experiences.

This chapter is a vital introduction to Japanese, focusing on equipping you with one of the fundamental scripts in the language – hiragana. Hiragana is a set of phonetic symbols that are mainly used for grammatical particles, as well as the endings of verbs and adjectives.

So why wait? Start your Japanese language journey today and discover all the thrilling possibilities that await you!

****
+++<svg style="width:4em;height:4em;"><use href="#goal"/></svg>+++
**Learning goals**

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

•  Understand what hiragana is and how it functions.
•  Understand the benefits of learning hiragana.
•  Identify and read the 46 basic hiragana characters.
•  Read some simple Japanese words that use hiragana characters.
•  Set up a Japanese keyboard input on your computer.
•  Type in Japanese.
****


*Figures*

[[c01_1]]
== 1.1 Introduction to Hiragana

In this module, we will cover the fundamentals of hiragana, focusing on effective reading techniques.

=== What is hiragana?

Hiragana is one of the three scripts in the Japanese writing system, as shown below:

1. *Hiragana (ひらがな)*
2. *Katakana (カタカナ)*
3. *Kanji (漢字)*

Hiragana is often the starting point in learning Japanese, primarily serving to provide grammatical context and connect kanji characters in sentences.

You can think of it as the ‘glue’ that holds words and sentences together, making it easier to read in Japanese.

In modern Japanese writing, all three scripts are used together in sentences. For example:

****
[cols="2a,2a"]
|===
| 私**は**今日、日本語**の**クラス**でひらがなを**勉強**します**。 +
 _Watashi *wa* kyou Nihongo *no* kurasu *de* hiragana *o* benkyou *shimasu*._
| I will study hiragana in Japanese class today.
|===

NOTE: The punctuation mark ‘、’ is equivalent to the English comma ‘,’. You will learn more about Japanese punctuation in <<c02_6, Module 2.6>>.
****

Here’s a brief overview of the distinct roles each script plays in the Japanese language. Please note that these are guidelines and exceptions do exist:

•  *Kanji* characters are used to represent certain words or parts of words. They are usually content words such as nouns, adjective stems and verb stems. Each kanji character has its own meaning and one or more readings (ways to pronounce it), which you will learn in Chapter 7. For example, 勉強 is a noun–verb compound made up of two kanji characters, 勉 (meaning ‘exertion’) and 強 (meaning ‘strong’). Together, they represent the concept of ‘study’ or ‘learning’. In Japanese, many verbs are formed by combining a kanji noun with the verb する (suru: to do), making it a verb phrase.
•  *Hiragana* characters are used to provide grammatical information and fill in the gaps between kanji characters.
•  *Katakana* characters are primarily used for transcribing foreign words from languages other than Chinese and are sometimes employed for emphasis. You will learn katakana in <<c04_1, Module 4.1>>.

Keep in mind that hiragana is phonetic, which means the same sentence in the above example can be written entirely in hiragana like this:

わたしはきょう、にほんごのくらすでひらがなをべんきょうします。

Hiragana is the starting block for learning Japanese, offering a simpler way to get the hang of reading and writing before diving into the more intricate kanji characters. However, reading sentences fully in hiragana can be tough, as it lacks the clear word boundaries that kanji and katakana provide.

For example, deciphering にわにはにわのにわとりがいる might seem daunting, but with kanji, it’s clearer: 庭には二羽の鶏がいる (niwa ni wa niwa no niwatori ga iru), which can be translated as ‘There are two chickens in the garden’. Here, kanji characters help to differentiate ‘in the garden’ (庭には: niwa ni wa) from ‘two chickens’ (二羽の鶏: niwa no niwatori), making the sentence much easier to comprehend and highlighting the importance of using different scripts for clarity.

Kanji will be introduced later in the textbook; let’s focus on the role of hiragana in Japanese for now.

=== How many hiragana characters are there?

Hiragana consists of *a set of 46 basic characters* in the Japanese writing system. While there are additional hiragana characters, which we will explore later in <<c02_2, Module 2.2>>, for now, let’s focus on these 46 fundamental ones.

=== How can I read words in hiragana?

Reading hiragana is not only straightforward but also quite enjoyable! Every hiragana character represents *a syllable*, akin to a small sound unit that forms words. These syllables can be just a single vowel sound (like the hiragana character あ for the sound ‘a’) or a combination of a consonant and a vowel (like the hiragana character か for the sound ‘ka’).

Now, for the fun part: when pronouncing these characters, treat each one as a *mora, a phonological unit used in some languages, including Japanese*. This approach ensures you give each character approximately the same amount of time and rhythm in your speech.

Take にほんご (Nihongo: Japanese language), for example, which comprises four moras (Ni-ho-n-go), each pronounced with equal duration. It’s similar to following a steady beat in music, adding a rhythmic aspect to speaking Japanese.

If you’re interested in learning more about moras and the pronunciation of Japanese vowels and consonants, I recommend checking out <<c02_1, Module 2.1>>.

Some examples of Japanese syllables include:

•  _ka_ in the word _kami_ (hair or paper) 
https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/Chapter-I-kami.m4a[icon:play-circle[]]

•  _shi_ in the word _shika_ (deer) 
https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/Chapter-I-shika.m4a[icon:play-circle[]]

•  _nu_ in the word _inu_ (dog) 
https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/Chapter-I-inu.m4a[icon:play-circle[]]

•  _re_ in the word _remon_ (lemon) 
https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/Chapter-I-remon.m4a[icon:play-circle[]]

•  _ho_ in the word _hoshi_ (star) 
https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/Chapter-I-hoshi.m4a[icon:play-circle[]]


=== Should I memorise all the hiragana characters now to read the book?

The answer is ‘no’. There is no need to memorise all the hiragana characters to read this book at this stage. Instead, we will use the Roman alphabet to represent Japanese sounds phonetically.

*Roomaji or rōmaji* (ローマ字: ろおまじ), also known as ‘romaji’ in English, is a useful tool for writing and reading Japanese, especially when you are not yet familiar with the three scripts.

For example, in Japan, you will often see romaji on signboards at train stations, as shown in Figure 1:

._Figure 1: Hibarigaoka Station Signboard_
image::https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/Tozai-line_Hibarigaoka-sta_Station-name_signboard-300x104.jpg[Train station signboard with place names written in both romanji and hiragana,width=421,height=146]


==== Romaji, Rōmaji and Romanisation: What’s the Difference?

•  *Romanisation* refers to the *general process* of transliterating languages into the Roman alphabet, not just Japanese.
•  *Roomaji or rōmaji* (with a macron) is the accurate *romanisation of the Japanese term ローマ字*, written in katakana and kanji, meaning ‘Roman letters’.
•  *Romaji* is the commonly used term in English for Japanese text transliterated into the Roman alphabet.

In short, *romaji* helps English speakers who are not familiar with the Japanese writing system *read and understand Japanese words more easily!*

'''''

Here is a downloadable PDF chart of basic hiragana that you can use to enhance your everyday Japanese learning experience. The chart is read from top to bottom, and left to right.

==== Basic hiragana chart 

[cols=",,,,,,,,,",]
|===
|あ (a) |か (ka) |さ (sa)  |た (ta) |な (na) |は (ha) |ま (ma) |や (ya) |ら (ra) |わ (wa)
|い (i) |き (ki) |し (shi) |ち (chi/ti) |に (ni) |ひ (hi) |み (mi) | |り (ri) |
|う (u) |く (ku) |す (su) |つ (tsu) |ぬ (nu) |ふ (fu/hu) |む (mu) |ゆ (yu) |る (ru) |を (o)
|え (e) |け (ke) |せ (se) |て (te) |ね (ne) |へ (he) |め (me) | |れ (re) |
|お (o) |こ (ko) |そ (so) |と (to) |の (no) |ほ (ho) |も (mo) |よ (yo) |ろ (ro) |ん (n)
|===

icon:exclamation-circle[2x] 
Please note that in this textbook, ち and ふ are represented as _chi_ and _fu_, respectively, following the Hepburn system of romanisation. However, in some other romanisation systems, they might be represented as _ti_ and _hu,_ respectively.

https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/Basic-Hiragana-Chart-Japanese-Introductory-1-3.pdf[Basic Hiragana Chart Japanese Introductory 1 (PDF 62 KB)]

==== Typing in Japanese chart 

Curious about typing in Japanese on your computer? Whether you’re a pro or just starting out, we’ve got you covered!

If you don’t have a Japanese keyboard or aren’t sure how to use one, don’t worry. Take a look at our easy-to-follow instructions below. They’ll guide you through setting up Japanese input on your computer and show you the basics of typing in Japanese.

https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/How-to-Set-Up-Japanese-Keyboard-Input-Japanese-Introductory-1-1.pdf[How to Set Up Japanese Keyboard Input Japanese Introductory 1 (PDF 168 KB)]


=== What are the key benefits of learning hiragana?

Here are some good reasons why learning hiragana offers a good foundation for your Japanese language studies:

•  Hiragana forms the core of the Japanese writing system. It is often used to represent sentence elements for which there are no kanji characters. It can also be used instead of kanji to transcribe a word phonetically. So, by mastering hiragana, you will be able to read and write most words in Japanese.
•  Hiragana serves as a foundation for learning the two other scripts, katakana and kanji. Knowing hiragana will make it easier for you to build your reading and writing skills in Japanese.
•  Learning hiragana will help you to improve your pronunciation of Japanese words. Each hiragana character corresponds to a specific sound (or mora). So, by learning hiragana, you will be able to pronounce words clearly and appropriately.
•  Last, but not least, learning hiragana can give you a sense of accomplishment! It can be highly motivating and boost your confidence in your language-learning abilities.

In sum, learning hiragana is a great first step for those starting out in Japanese. It creates a solid base for comprehending, writing and reading the language, and it is crucial for communicating effectively in Japanese.

=== Further resources

To further enhance your understanding of the benefits of learning hiragana, the following resource (about 3 minutes long) created by https://www.youtube.com/@Tofugu[Tofugu] may be useful. It also provides you with an overview of how hiragana functions, which will be covered in more detail in the subsequent modules in this chapter:

Wikimedia Commons. Licensed under https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/[CC by SA 4.0].


[[c01_2]]
== 1.2 あ (a) – こ (ko)

This module will guide you through the essentials of reading hiragana characters. We will focus on 10 of the 46 basic hiragana characters: あ (a), い (i), う (u), え (e) and お (o), as well as か (ka), き (ki), く (ku), け (ke) and こ (ko).

The five vowel sounds of hiragana – あ (a, as in ‘uh’), い (i, as in ‘six’), う (u, as in ‘put’), え (e, as in ‘met’) and お (o, as in ‘pot’) – are essential. Remember to pronounce them crisply and briefly. They pair with consonants like ‘k’, ‘s’, ‘t’, ‘n’, ‘h’, ‘m’, ‘y’, ‘r’ and ‘w’, forming the basis of the hiragana script. This combination of sounds is vital for mastering the reading and pronunciation of Japanese.

=== 1. あ (a), い (i), う (u),  え (e),  お (o)

Practise saying each character slowly at first, and then increase the speed. After that, challenge yourself to pronounce all five characters smoothly in one go, as if in a single breath.
https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/a-i-u-e-o.m4a[icon:play-circle[]]

For a focused exploration of the Japanese vowel sounds あ, い, う, え and お, <<c02_1, Module 2.1>> offers comprehensive guidance and is a great resource.


Are you a visual learner? Then you may find *mnemonics* useful.

But what exactly is mnemonics?

Mnemonics  is a learning strategy that assists in the process of memorisation and recall. It can take the form of images, songs, rhymes or other creative strategies.

To learn the 46 basic hiragana characters, you can also refer to this resource (about 3 minutes long) created by https://www.youtube.com/@Tofugu[Tofugu]. It shows how mnemonics can be used to learn あ, い, う, え and お:

https://www.youtube.com/embed/WJdwOpJwpYg?feature=oembed&rel=0[Learn Hiragana Today #2: あいうえお]
// video::WJdwOpJwpYg[youtube]

'''''

The characters か (ka), き (ki), く (ku), け (ke) and こ (ko) are our next focus.


=== 2. か (ka), き (ki), く (ku), け (ke), こ (ko)

Again, practise saying each character slowly at first, and then increase the speed. After that, challenge yourself to pronounce all five characters smoothly in one go, as if in a single breath.
https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/ka-ki-ku-ke-ko.m4a[icon:play-circle[]]

For a deeper dive into Japanese consonant pronunciation, <<c02_1, Module 2.1>> is highly recommended. It provides comprehensive insights and practical examples to help you master the sounds effectively.


Here is an additional resource (about 3 minutes long) created by https://www.youtube.com/@Tofugu[Tofugu], which shows you how to learn か, き, く, け and こ using mnemonics:

https://www.youtube.com/embed/gLPzqSkIH58?feature=oembed&rel=0[Learn Hiragana Today #3: かきくけこ]
// video::gLPzqSkIH58[youtube]

*References*

1. “Learn Hiragana Today #2: あいうえお.” YouTube video, 3:29. August 12, 2014. https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WJdwOpJwpYg.

2. “Learn Hiragana Today #3: かきくけこ.” YouTube video, 3:20. August 12, 2014. https://youtu.be/gLPzqSkIH58?si=BrpKE0GPTeWUNCLt.


[[c01_3]]
== 1.3 さ (sa) – と (to)

In this module, we will focus on the hiragana characters さ (sa), し (shi), す (su), せ (se) and そ (so), along with た (ta), ち (chi), つ (tsu), て (te) and と (to).

=== 1. さ (sa), し (shi), す (su), せ (se), そ (so)

Practise saying each character slowly at first, and then increase the speed. After that, challenge yourself to pronounce all five characters smoothly in one go, as if in a single breath.
https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/sa-shi-su-se-so.m4a[icon:play-circle[]]

2.  Some hiragana characters look very similar. Can you figure out if the following statement is true or false?

1. Is the hiragana 'ko' written as に?  [.QA]*False (ko is こ)*
2. Is the hiragana 'ki' written as き?  [.QA]*True (ki is き)*
3. Is the hiragana 'shi' written as も?  [.QA]*False (shi is し)*
4. Is the hiragana 'ra' written as ち?  [.QA]*True (ra is ら)*
5. Is the hiragana 'ne' written as ね?  [.QA]*True (ne is ね)*
6. Is the hiragana 'wa' written as は?  [.QA]*True (wa is わ)*


NOTE: The hiragana 'wa' is written as わ. However, it is important to note that when the hiragana character は is used as a particle, it is pronounced as 'wa' instead of its usual 'ha' sound.

The following resource, created by https://www.youtube.com/@TOMOsensei[TOMO sensei], contains additional words in hiragana. You may find it helpful for assessing your understanding of hiragana:

https://www.youtube.com/embed/EwJgubX_J4k?feature=oembed&rel=0[HIRAGANA TEST 01 - Japanese Words Quiz: Hiragana Reading Practice for Beginners]
// video::EwJgubX_J4k[youtube]


=== What is the difference between お (o) and を (0)？

In Japanese, the hiragana character お (o) serves multiple purposes in vocabulary.

Firstly, it is commonly used in everyday words, like おおさか (Oosaka: Osaka) and おとな (otona: adults, grown-ups).

Additionally, it functions as an honorific prefix for nouns, conveying politeness and respect. For example, in おちゃ (ocha: tea), お acts as the honorific prefix for _cha_ (tea). Without the honorific お, the tone of what you are saying can shift from polite to brusque.

In contrast , を (o) is a particle used to mark the direct object of a sentence – the target of an action or the object that is being acted upon. For example, in the sentence 「おちゃをください。」(ocha o kudasai), which means ‘Tea, please’ or, more literally, ‘Please give me tea,’ を marks おちゃ as the object being given.

You will learn more about the particle を in <<c02_5, Module 2.5>>.

=== How should I pronounce ん (n)?

In Japanese, the character ん (n) is distinct from the English ‘n’ in terms of pronunciation. It represents a nasal sound and is produced through the nose rather than the mouth.

Here are a few tips for practising the pronunciation of ん:

•  Listen to native Japanese speakers and try to imitate their pronunciation.
•  Practise saying words that contain ん out loud, such as せんせい (sensei: teacher) and にほん (Nihon: Japan).
•  Drop your jaw slightly as you articulate the Japanese ん sound to pronounce it accurately.

The pronunciation of ん can vary slightly depending on the surrounding characters. At this stage, it is not necessary to master these nuances, but if you are interested in delving deeper into the pronunciation of ん, please see the following resource created by https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3M-2LjAWb3M[Dogen].

=== How can I write hiragana?

To write hiragana, start with the basic vowels: あ, い, う, え and お. Learn each character’s stroke order to make sure you know the proper form and structure.

Consistent practice is key to achieving smooth, natural handwriting. Online resources, like those from the Japan Foundation, offer valuable guidance. They provide hiragana worksheets to help you learn the stroke order and practise writing the characters. Check out these resources at the https://classroomresources.sydney.jpf.go.jp/resources/hiragana-memory-hint-worksheets/[Japan Foundation’s website].


*References*

1. Dogen. “The 7 Different Ways to Pronounce ん / 「ん」の7つの音.” YouTube video, 1:36. November 16, 2021. https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3M-2LjAWb3M[https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3M-2LjAWb3M.]

2. Japan Foundation. “Hiragana Memory Hint Worksheets.” October, 2020. https://classroomresources.sydney.jpf.go.jp/resources/hiragana-memory-hint-worksheets//.

3. Learn Japanese with Kobachan-tachi. “[Hiragana] Learn Japanese—Hiragana R-Line らりるれろ in 15 Minutes.” YouTube video, 14:53. April 5, 2023. https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=R00ytkEZ7_4&t=2s&ab_channel=LearnJapanesewithKobachan-tachi[https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=R00ytkEZ7_4&t=2s&ab_channel=LearnJapanesewithKobachan-tachi.]

April 8, 2023. https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QW_tz795DHI&ab_channel=LearnJapanesewithKobachan-tachi[https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QW_tz795DHI&ab_channel=LearnJapanesewithKobachan-tachi.]

= Chapter 2: Welcome to Japanese Learning Communities (1)

[[c02_0]]
== 2.0 Welcome to Japanese-Learning Communities (1)

._Figure 1: Tokyo Sky_
[.text-center]
[link=https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/pexels-pierre-blache-6058051.jpg]
image::https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/pexels-pierre-blache-6058051-1024x576.jpg[Tokyo Sky,width=1007,height=567]

In this book, our goal is to provide you with the tools and knowledge to actively participate in dynamic language-learning communities.

In ‘Welcome to Japanese-Learning Communities’, you will start a fun journey to learn basic Japanese conversation skills. This chapter will show you how to introduce yourself and help you make friends with your fellow classmates and other members in language-learning communities.

It’s designed to make interactions in Japanese straightforward and enjoyable, whether you’re greeting someone for the first time or stepping into a new cross-cultural sphere.

****
+++<svg style="height:4em;width:4em;"><use href="#goal"/></svg>+++
*Learning goals*

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

•  Identify additional hiragana characters.
•  Use simple greetings to start a friendly conversation in Japanese.
•  Understand and apply the fundamental sentence pattern ‘X は (wa) Y です (desu)’ to say ‘X is Y’ in a self-introduction.
•  Learn the Japanese punctuation system to enhance your reading and writing skills.
•  Gain insights into various first-person pronouns in Japanese, aside from 私 (わたし: watashi), and how to use them.

+++<svg style="height:4em;width:4em;"><use href="#lightbulb"/></svg>+++
*Why these skills matter*

*Cognitive benefits*

•  Strengthen pattern recognition and reading fluency
•  Build confidence in spoken interaction and cultural adaptability
•  Reinforce logical sentence structure for clearer expression
•  Enhance reading comprehension through punctuation
•  Develop awareness of social nuance in pronoun use

*Real-life benefits*

•  Start friendly conversations with ease
•  Introduce yourself clearly and naturally
•  Use appropriate pronouns for better communication
****

=== Words

[cols="^1,^1,^1,<3",]
|===
|*Japanese* |*Romaji* |*English* |*Notes*
|私（わたし） |_watashi_ |I/me |This is a formal and gender-neutral first-person pronoun. For more information on gender and first-person pronouns, please see <<c06_7, Module 6.7>>.
|名前（なまえ） |_namae_ |name |
|お名前 |_onamae_ |name |The honorific prefix お (o) is used before 名前 (namae) to show politeness. It is typically used to refer to someone else’s name, not your own name. For further details on honorific prefixes, please see <<c01_6, Module 1.6>>.
|先生（せんせい） |_sensei_ |teacher/professor/doctor/ lawyer/author, etc. |This is also an honorific title placed after the family name of the person to whom you are referring.
|… さん |_san_ |Mr/Ms/Mrs or any non-binary titles |The honorific さん (san) is a gender-neutral title used after someone’s family name or given name. It is only used when referring to others, not oneself.
|皆（みな）さん |_mina-san_ |everyone |Polite/formal
|学生（がくせい） |_gakusei_ |student |This word usually refers to university students.
|大学（だいがく） |_daigaku_ |university |
|モナシュ（もなしゅ） |_Monashu_ |Monash |
|自己紹介（じこしょうかい） |_jiko-shoukai_ |self-introduction |自己 (jiko) means ‘self’. 紹介 (shoukai) means ‘introduction’ or ‘referral’.
|日本（にほん） |_Nihon_ |Japan |
|日本語（にほんご） |_Nihongo_ |Japanese language |This refers to the Japanese language. The suffix 語 (go) is used to indicate a language, so 日本語 combines 日本 (Japan) with 語, meaning ‘Japanese language’. This structure applies to other languages as well. For example, the term for English is 英語（えいご: Eigo）. Here, 英 (Ei) refers to ‘England’ or ‘English’, and 語 (go) again indicates a language, so 英語 means ‘English language’. Similarly, you can use 語 with other countries to refer to their languages, such as フランス語（ふらんすご: Furansugo: French language) and 中国語 (ちゅうごくご: Chuugokugo: Chinese language).
|水（みず） |_mizu_ |water |
|===

=== Expressions and phrases

[cols="^1,^1,^1,<3",]
|===
|*Japanese* |*Romaji* |*English* |*Notes*
|じゃあ |_jaa_ |well then … / in that case … |A short response used to transition in a conversation or before indicating a decision.
|あ |_a_ |oh / ah / oh / I see. |A short response used to express realisation or understanding.
|ええと |_eeto_ |um … / Let’s see … |A short response used to convey hesitation while thinking.
|はい |_hai_ |yes/okay |Polite. はい (hai) is versatile in Japanese, commonly used as ‘yes’ to affirmatively respond to questions. Beyond just ‘yes’, it can imply ‘okay’, signalling agreement or acknowledgement in a conversation. It’s used to show that the speaker is listening and comprehending what another person is saying, often used to maintain the flow of conversation. Conversely, the term for ‘no’ in Japanese is いいえ (iie), which will be introduced in <<c03_0, Module 3.0>>.
|お願（ねが）いします |_onegai shimasu_ |please |This phrase can stand alone in requests or be used with a direct object.
|X（を）お願いします |_X (o) onegai shimasu_ |X, please |In this sentence pattern, the particle を (o) follows the noun (X), marking it as the direct object. In casual conversation, this particle can sometimes be omitted for brevity. Please see the ‘Key Grammar Points’ section below.
|はじめまして |_hajime mashite_ |Nice to meet you |Neutral
|よろしく |_yoroshiku_ |Treat me kindly; Nice to meet you |Informal (see link:#c02_3[Module 2.3)]
|よろしくお願いします |_yoroshiku onegai shimasu_ |Please treat me kindly; Nice to meet you |Polite/formal. To learn more about the distinctions between はじめまして and よろしくお願いします, please see <<c02_3, Module 2.3>>.
|ありがとう |_arigatou_ |Thanks |Informal
|ありがとうございます |_arigatou gozaimasu_ |Thank you very much |Polite/formal
|どういたしまして |_dou itashi mashite_ |You’re welcome |Neutral
|こんにちは |_konnichiwa_ |Hello |Neutral. Note that the hiragana character は is pronounced ‘wa’ rather than ‘ha’ (see link:#c02_3[Module 2.3)].
|おはよう |_ohayou_ |Morning [greeting] |Informal
|おはようございます |_ohayou gozaimasu_ |Good morning |Polite/formal
|さようなら |_sayounara_ |Bye |Polite/formal
|すみません |_sumimasen_ |Excuse me/I’m sorry [apology] |Polite/formal (see link:#c02_3[Module 2.3)]
|ごめんなさい |_gomennasai_ |I’m sorry [apology] |Polite/less formal (see link:#c02_3[Module 2.3)]
|とてもいいですね |_totemo ii desu ne_ |(That’s) really good. |Polite
|===

=== Key grammar points

[cols="^1,^1,^1,<3",]
|===
|*Japanese* |*Romaji* |*English* |*Notes*
|は |_wa_ |— |Particle. This hiragana character is usually pronounced ‘ha’, but when it is used as a particle it is pronounced ‘wa’. This particle is often referred to as a ‘topic marker’, used to indicate the topic of a sentence (see <<c02_4, Module 2.4>>).
|です |_desu_ |is, am, are |Copula/linking verb
|Xは? |_X wa_? |What about X? |In the given sentence pattern, ‘X’ represents a noun, and the particle は (wa) is used to mark the topic of the sentence. This usage highlights the noun ‘X’ as the main topic of discussion.
|を |_o_ |— |Particle. This particle is often referred to as an ‘object marker’, used to indicate the direct object of a verb (what or who is being acted upon; see <<c02_5, Module 2.5>>).
|===

=== Exercise 1

Guess which hiragana character needs to be filled in. To check the answer, click the ‘turn’ button. You can also listen to the sound of each word by clicking the audio icon.

You may notice that some words are not pronounced in the exact way they are written in hiragana and romaji. These sounds are called ‘long vowel’ sounds, which will be covered in more detail in <<c02_2, Module 2.2>>.

[[h5p-7]]
https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/japanese/wp-admin/admin-ajax.php?action=h5p_embed&id=7[HTML5 Package(h5p.org) - Chapter 2 Vocab]

1. 	わ 【 [.QA]*た* 】 し -- I
2. 	な 【 [.QA]*ま* 】 え -- name
3. 	がくせ 【 [.QA]*い* 】 -- students (at university)
4. 	【 [.QA]*は* 】 じめまして -- Nice to meet you
5. 	せんせ 【 [.QA]*い* 】 -- teacher
6. 	だ 【 [.QA]*い*  】 がく -- university
7. 	よ 【 [.QA]*ろ* 】 しく -- Treat me kindly
8. 	おはよ 【 [.QA]*う* 】 -- Greeting: 'Morning'
9. 	こんにち [　[.QA]*わ*] -- Greeting: 'Hello'
10. ありがと 【 [.QA]*う* 】 -- Thanks
11. ど 【 [.QA]*う* 】 いたしまして -- You're welcome
12. すみませ 【 [.QA]*ん* 】 -- Excuse me/I'm sorry
13. さよ 【 [.QA]*う* 】 なら -- Greeting: 'Bye'
14. わたし 【 [.QA]*は* 】 がくせいです。 -- I'm a student.
15. じこしょうかいを 【 [.QA]*お* 】 ねがいします。 -- Please introduce yourself.


****
+++<svg style="height:4em;width:4em;"><use href="#translation"/></svg>+++
*Model dialogue*

Lina and Kevin are practising self-introductions in their first Japanese language class at a university in Australia.

[width="100%",cols="50%,50%",]
|===
|*Teacher:* じゃあ、みなさん。自己紹介（じこしょうかい）をお願（ねが）いします。 |_Jaa, mina-san. Jiko-shoukai o onegai shimasu._
|*Lina:* あ、はじめまして。私（わたし）は * リナ（りな）です。よろしく。
[* The non-Japanese name ‘Lina’ is usually written in katakana, but we are providing the hiragana here to help you read it in Japanese.] |_A, hajime mashite. Watashi wa Rina desu. Yoroshiku._
|*Kevin:* Oh, so can we just say よろしく? |Oh, so can we just say, _yoroshiku_?
|*Lina:* Yeah, that’s a shorter and more casual version of _yoroshiku onegai shimasu_. |Yeah, that’s a shorter and more casual version of _yoroshiku onegai shimasu_.
|*Kevin:* Right. ありがとう、リナさん。 |Right. _Arigatou, Rina-san._
|*Lina:* ええと、お名前（なまえ）は？ |_Eeto, onamae wa?_
|*Kevin:* ケビン（けびん）です。はじめまして。よろしくお願い … What was that again? |_Kebin desu. Hajime mashite. Yoroshiku onegai …_ What was that again?
|*Lina:* よろしく is just fine! |_Yoroshiku_ is just fine!
|*Kevin:* よろしく！ |_Yoroshiku!_
|*Teacher:* リナさん、ケビンさん、とてもいいですね！Just quickly, when you’re talking to your teachers or people who are older than you or in more formal contexts, it’s customary to use the longer version of the phrase, ‘_yoroshiku onegai shimasu_’. |_Rina-san, Kebin-san, totemo ii desu ne! Just quickly, when you’re talking to your teachers or people who are older than you or in more formal contexts, it’s customary to use the longer version of the phrase, ‘yoroshiku onegai shimasu’._
|*Lina and Kevin:* はい。ありがとうございます！ |_Hai, arigatou gozaimasu!_
|*Teacher*: どういたしまして。 |_Dou itashi mashite._
|===

==== English translation

[width="100%",cols="100%",]
|===
|*Teacher:* All right, everyone. Please introduce yourselves [literally, ‘Self introductions, please’].
|*Lina:* Oh, nice to meet you. I’m Lina. _Yoroshiku_ [Treat me kindly].
|*Kevin:* Oh, so can we just say _yoroshiku_?
|*Lina*: Yeah, that’s a shorter and more casual version of _yoroshiku onegai shimasu_.
|*Kevin*: Right. Thanks, Lina.
|*Lina*: Well, (what’s) your name?
|*Kevin*: (I’m) Kevin. Nice to meet you. _Yoroshiku onegai_ … What was that again?
|*Lina*: _Yoroshiku_ is just fine!
|*Kevin*: _Yoroshiku_!
|*Teacher*: Lina and Kevin, that’s very good! Just quickly, when you’re talking to your teachers or people who are older than you or in more formal contexts, it’s customary to use the longer version of the phrase, _yoroshiku onegai shimasu_.
|*Lina and Kevin*: Okay. Thank you very much!
|*Teacher*: You’re welcome.
|===
****

**Discussion points**

Let’s share your observations and thoughts on the following points!

•  The greeting *よろしくお願いします* (yoroshiku onegai shimasu) might have sounded quite unfamiliar to you at first. How did it make you feel when you heard it for the first time? Is there an expression in your own language that conveys a similar meaning?
•  How do the brief expressions *あ (a)*, *じゃあ* *(jaa)* and *ええと* *(eeto)* function in the conversation? Can you think of specific situations where you might use these phrases? Are you familiar with any other short expressions that facilitate smoother dialogue?

=== Further resources

The following resource (about 2 minutes long), created by https://www.youtube.com/@japanvoices6578[Japan Voices], may help you understand the cultural nuances and connotations of よろしくお願いします. It uses scenes from the Studio Ghibli anime _Tonari no Totoro_ (_My Neighbour, Totoro)_, to illustrate the phrase’s use when the family moves to a new house in a new area:

https://www.youtube.com/embed/eB9nMSQXFKY?feature=oembed&rel=0[The true meaning of Yoroshiku onegai shimasu from My Neighbor Totoro (2)]
// video::eB9nMSQXFKY[youtube]

[[c02_1]]
== 2.1 The Japanese Sound System

In this module, we will explore the fundamental aspects of the Japanese sound system.

The Japanese sound system is relatively straightforward compared to that of English. Each syllable consists of a consonant and a vowel.


=== Japanese vowels

The Japanese sound system has *five vowels*, namely, ‘a’, ‘i’, ‘u’, ‘e’ and ‘o’.

Let’s watch this short video (about 1 minute long) created by https://www.youtube.com/@kokorocommunications[Kokoro Communications] to learn and practise how to pronounce Japanese vowels:

https://www.youtube.com/embed/JOaJ2BFq-xs?feature=oembed&rel=0[Japanese Phonetics Part 1: Vowel Sounds #shorts]
// video::JOaJ2BFq-xs[youtube]

Each vowel sound is pronounced distinctly and clearly. For example, ‘a’ is pronounced as /ah/. It is somewhat akin to the ‘uh’ sound in English words like ‘cup’, ‘study’ and ‘tunnel’. However, remember to keep it short and not stretch out the sound.

=== Japanese consonants

These consonants are combined with the vowels to form syllables. Compared to English, the Japanese pronunciation system is relatively consistent and uncomplicated.

Japanese consonants are made up of a limited number of sounds, such as ‘k’, ‘s’, ‘t’, ‘n’, ‘h’, ‘m’, ‘y’, ‘r’ and ‘w’, with some variations of these sounds. They are combined with the five vowels to form syllables.

Let’s watch this short video (about 1 minute long), created by https://www.youtube.com/@kokorocommunications[Kokoro Communications], to learn and practise how to pronounce Japanese consonants:

Please note that the chart displayed behind the presenter is oriented vertically. In vertical writing, you read from right to left and from top to bottom. Consequently, the initial hiragana character ‘あ’ (a) is located in the top right corner:

https://www.youtube.com/embed/LWAC224J-SI?feature=oembed&rel=0[Japanese Phonetics Part 2: Consonants #shorts]
// video::LWAC224J-SI[youtube]

'''''

In <<c02_2, the next module>>, we will explore more about the Japanese sound system using hiragana!

[[c02_2]]
== 2.2 Additional Features of Hiragana

In this module, we will explore four additional features of hiragana and how they reflect the pronunciation of words in Japanese.

There are four additional features of hiragana that influence the pronunciation of words in Japanese:

1. *Long vowels*
2. *Diacritical marks*
3. *Contracted sounds*
4. *Double consonants*

Let’s explore each of these features in detail.

=== 1. Long vowels

Some vowels in Japanese can be pronounced as long vowel sounds, which are represented by hiragana. For example, the vowel ‘e’ is long, pronounced as /ee/, in the word せんせい (sensei). In hiragana, it is represented by a せ (se) and an い (i).

=== Exercise 1

=== 2. Diacritical marks

Some hiragana characters can be modified with diacritical marks to represent different sounds. There are two types of diacritical marks: *1) だくてん (dakuten)* and *2) はんだくてん (han-dakuten)*.

*1) だくてん (dakuten) :  ゛*

The _dakuten_ is represented by two small lines that look like a double quotation mark (゛). It can be placed to the right of certain characters.

A _dakuten_ is a mark that alters the sound of a Japanese character from soft (unvoiced) to harder (voiced). For example, the character か (ka) has a soft sound, where the ‘k’ is spoken without vibrating the vocal cords. When you add a _dakuten_ to か, it becomes が (ga), changing the sound to a harder ‘g’, where the vocal cords vibrate.

Please listen to the recording provided below and notice the difference between the unvoiced ‘k’ sounds and the voiced ‘g’ sounds in these pairs of characters: か/が (ka/ga), き/ぎ (ki/gi), く/ぐ (ku/gu), け/げ (ke/ge) and こ/ご (ko/go).

Pay attention to how the ‘k’ sounds are made without vocal cord vibration, while the ‘g’ sounds are made with vocal cord vibration.
https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/Chapter-2-dakuten-sounds-gagigugego.m4a[icon:play-circle[]]


*2) はんだくてん (han-dakuten) :  ゜*

The _han-dakuten_ is represented by a small circle (゜) and can be placed to the right of an H-sound character.

A _han-dakuten_ modifies a character by changing the sound of its initial consonant ‘h’ to a ‘p’. For example, adding a _han-dakuten_ to は (ha) changes it to ぱ (pa).

Here is a chart of hiragana with _dakuten_ (゛) and _han-dakuten_ (゜). Remember to read the chart from top to bottom, and left to right.

Note that ぢ is represented as _ji_ and づ is represented as _zu_ in this textbook, following the Hepburn system of romanisation. But you might also see them represented as _di_ and _du_ respectively in some places, depending on the romaji system used.

[%autowidth, cols=",,,,", role="big",]
|===
|が (ga) |ざ (za) |だ (da)    |ば (ba) |ぱ (pa)
|ぎ (gi) |じ (ji) |ぢ (ji/di) |び (bi) |ぴ (pi)
|ぐ (gu) |ず (zu) |づ (zu/du) |ぶ (bu) |ぷ (pu)
|げ (ge) |ぜ (ze) |で (de)    |べ (be) |ぺ (pe)
|ご (go) |ぞ (zo) |ど (do)    |ぼ (bo) |ぽ (po)
|===

Please listen to the recording below to hear the pronunciation of each hiragana character, ranging from が (ga) to ぽ (po):
https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/Chapter-2-dakuten-handakuten-chart.m4a[icon:play-circle[]]

=== 3. Contracted sounds

Two syllables in Japanese can merge into a single, contracted sound, known as *拗音（ようおん, you-on)*. In hiragana, this contraction is represented by adding small versions of *ゃ (ya), ゅ (yu) or ょ (yo)* to characters ending in ‘i’, excluding the character い (i) itself. This process effectively replaces the ‘i’ sound with ‘ya’, ‘yu’ or ‘yo’. For example, combining き (ki) with a small ゃ (ya) changes it from き (ki) to きゃ (kya), creating this contracted sound.

Here is a chart of the contracted sounds in hiragana:

[%autowidth, cols=",,,,,,", role="big",]
|===
|きゃ (kya) |しゃ (sha) |ちゃ (cha) |にゃ (nya) |ひゃ (hya) |みゃ (mya) |りゃ (rya)
|きゅ (kyu) |しゅ (shu) |ちゅ (chu) |にゅ (nyu) |ひゅ (hyu) |みゅ (myu) |りゅ (ryu)
|きょ (kyo) |しょ (shu) |ちょ (cho) |にょ (nyo) |ひょ (hyo) |みょ (myo) |りょ (ryo)
|===

Please listen to the recording below to hear the pronunciation of each hiragana character, ranging from きゃ (kya) to りょ (ryo):
https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/Chapter-2-contracted-sounds-chart.m4a[icon:play-circle[]]

=== 4. Double consonants

Japanese has some sounds that are pronounced as a double consonant, and hiragana characters are used to transcribe these sounds.

Double consonants is represented by *a small っ (tsu)*, called *促音 (そくおん, soku-on)*. It introduce a brief pause before the following consonant sound, creating a distinct pronunciation effect akin to a stutter or a stronger emphasis on the following consonant.

For example, the word 納豆 (なっとう, nattou), meaning ‘fermented soybeans’, is written with a small っ (tsu) character between the な (na) and と (to) characters to indicate that the ‘t’ is pronounced as a double consonant ‘tt’.

=== Exercise 4

[[h5p-72]]
https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/japanese/wp-admin/admin-ajax.php?action=h5p_embed&id=72[HTML5 Package(h5p.org) - Chapter 2 Double Consonants (revised)]

'''''

Voiced consonants, contracted sounds and double consonants can be combined in various ways to represent a wide range of sounds in Japanese. It is important to learn these different combinations and their pronunciations to read and write Japanese effectively.

Here are some charts of hiragana with additional features to enhance your daily hiragana practice:

=== Additional hiragana charts

*Hiragana with diacritical marks*

[%autowidth, cols=",,,,", role="big",]
|===
|が (ga) |ざ (za) |だ (da)    |ば (ba) |ぱ (pa)
|ぎ (gi) |じ (ji) |ぢ (ji/di) |び (bi) |ぴ (pi)
|ぐ (gu) |ず (zu) |づ (zu/du) |ぶ (bu) |ぷ (pu)
|げ (ge) |ぜ (ze) |で (de)    |べ (be) |ぺ (pe)
|ご (go) |ぞ (zo) |ど (do)    |ぼ (bo) |ぽ (po)
|===


*Hiragana for contracted sounds*

[%autowidth, cols=",,,,,,", role="big",]
|===
|きゃ (kya) |しゃ (sha) |ちゃ (cha) |にゃ (nya) |ひゃ (hya) |みゃ (mya) |りゃ (rya)
|きゅ (kyu) |しゅ (shu) |ちゅ (chu) |にゅ (nyu) |ひゅ (hyu) |みゅ (myu) |りゅ (ryu)
|きょ (kyo) |しょ (shu) |ちょ (cho) |にょ (nyo) |ひょ (hyo) |みょ (myo) |りょ (ryo)
|===

NOTE: In addition to the standard set of additional hiragana, there are a few characters that are not commonly used in standard Japanese but can be found in certain contexts. These include: てぃ (ti), でぃ (di), ふぁ (fa) and ふぉ (fo).

These characters are typically used in loanwords and foreign names. They represent sounds that are not traditionally part of the Japanese phonetic system but have been adopted to accommodate foreign words and sounds. For example, ふぁみりー (famirii) for ‘family’ (usually written in hatakana as ファミリー) or でぃずにー (dizunii) for ‘Disney’ (also usually written in katakana as ディズニー). These characters showcase the adaptability of the Japanese writing system in incorporating foreign linguistic elements.

*Hiragana with diacritical marks and contracted sounds*

[%autowidth, cols=",,,,", role="big",]
|===
|ぎゃ (gya) |じゃ (ja) |ぢゃ (ja/dya) |びゃ (bya) |ぴゃ (pya)
|ぎゅ (gyu) |じゅ (ju) |ぢゅ (ju/dyu) |びゅ (byu) |ぴゅ (pyu)
|ぎょ (gyo) |じょ (jo) |ぢょ (jo/dyo) |びょ (byo) |ぴょ (pyo)
|===

Please listen to the pronunciation of each character listed in the table:
https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/Chapter-2-diacritical-marks-and-contracted-sounds-chart.m4a[icon:play-circle[]]

The combined chart of hiragana with additional features can be downloaded here:

https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/app/uploads/sites/67/2023/06/Additional-Hiragana-Chart-Japanese-Introductory-1-2.pdf[Additional Hiragana Chart Japanese Introductory 1 (PDF 75 KB)]

'''''

Now that you have familiarised yourself with the basic hiragana characters and their additional features, you might be interested in  the following comprehensive resource created by https://www.youtube.com/@JapanesePod101[JapanesePod101.com].

[[c02_3]]
== 2.3 The Art of Meeting and Greeting

In this module, you will learn how to introduce yourself politely and when to use common greetings, while exploring the cultural nuances and the usage of the honorific suffix さん (san).

=== How can I introduce myself in Japanese?

There is no set formula for introducing yourself in Japanese, as it can vary depending on the situation and personal preferences. However, you can try this commonly used, straightforward set of expressions for introducing yourself in Japanese:


==== Step 1. はじめまして

はじめまして (hajime mashite) is a polite and friendly way to greet someone for the first time. It literally means ‘This is the beginning’ and can be roughly translated as ‘Nice to meet you’ in English.

==== Step 2. Your name + です

Next, say your name followed by です (desu). For example, リナ（りな）です (Rina desu) means ‘(I am) Lina’.

You can also add the pronoun 私（わたし: watashi: I) and the topic particle は (pronounced ‘wa’, not ‘ha’). For example, 私はリナです means ‘I am Lina’.

We will learn more about how to use the ‘X は Yです’ sentence structure in <<c02_4, Module 2.4>>.

It is important to remember not to use さん (san) with your own name. It is an honorific suffix that is intended for others as a sign of politeness and respect, not for self-reference.

==== Step 3. よろしくお願（ねが）いします

よろしくお願いします (yoroshiku onegai shimasu) is a phrase used to express your desire for a good relationship with the person you are speaking to. It can be translated as ‘Please treat me kindly’ or ‘Nice to meet you’ in this context.

In a casual situation, such as a class activity with peers, you can use a shorter, more casual version: _yoroshiku_. However, it is important to remember that it is not appropriate to use this shorter version to address teachers or people who are older than you.

To put it all together, you can say:

[cols=">5a,4a",frame=all,grid=none]
|===
| *はじめまして。(私は) リナです。よろしくお願いします。* +
 _Hajime mashite. (Watashi wa) Rina desu. Yoroshiku onegai shimasu._
| Nice  to meet you. (I am) Lina. Please treat me kindly.
|===

Introducing yourself in Japanese can be a bit intimidating, but don’t worry! With these simple phrases and a friendly smile, you can make a great impression.

=== Exercise 1

Introduce yourself by filling in your name in the blank in the following sample sentences and record your voice. Afterward, listen to your recording to ensure that your pronunciation is clear and easy to understand.

[[h5p-6]]
https://oercollective.caul.edu.au/japanese/wp-admin/admin-ajax.php?action=h5p_embed&id=6[HTML5 Package(h5p.org) - Hiragana Reading: Self-introduction]

はじめまして。(Hajime mashite) わたしは ＿＿＿＿ です。(Watashi wa ＿＿＿＿ desu) よろしくおねがいします。(Yoroshiku onegai shimasu)

In addition to these self-introduction sentences, bowing is an important aspect of the greeting process in Japan. The timing of the bow may vary, but it is typical to bow while aying よろしくお願いします (yoroshiku onegai shimasu).

Here, it’s important to keep your eyes down while bowing. This contrasts with the Western practice of shaking hands, where maintaining eye contact is seen as polite and indicative of honesty. In Japanese culture, bowing with your eyes lowered is a sign of humility; direct eye contact during a bow can be perceived as aggressive or disrespectful.

'''''

If you’re interested in practising the pronunciation of these self-introduction sentences with the correct pitch – which refers to the variation in the tone and how high or low the voice is – the following resource created by https://www.youtube.com/@SpeakJapaneseNaturally[Speak Japanese Naturally] may be helpful.

https://www.youtube.com/embed/UsaNCx8Xzl0?feature=oembed&#38;rel=0[How to introduce yourself in Japanese with pitch accent | 自己紹介（じこしょうかい）]
// video::UsaNCx8Xzl0[youtube]

'''''

The expression よろしくお願いします, or simply よろしく in more casual situations, is incredibly versatile. It is used in various contexts, including when asking for a favour.

=== What is the difference between はじめまして and よろしくお願いします?

はじめまして (hajime mashite) is used when meeting someone for the first time, equivalent to ‘Nice to meet you’ in English. This greeting expresses that it’s the beginning of your acquaintance.

よろしくお願いします (yoroshiku onegai shimasu) is harder to translate directly but can mean ‘Please be kind to me’, ‘I’m looking forward to working with you’ or ‘Thank you in advance’. It’s used after introductions to request favourable treatment or to express anticipation of a good relationship.

So, はじめまして is used to introduce oneself, while よろしくお願いします requests a positive interaction moving forward.

=== What are the other simple greetings and expressions I can use?

Here are some simple Japanese greetings and expressions that you can use in both formal and casual settings:

==== こんにちは (konnichiwa)

This is a common greeting that means ‘Hello’ or ‘Good afternoon’. It can be used in both formal and casual settings. The は in こんにちは is pronounced _wa_ instead of _ha_ because it is used here as a particle that marks the topic of the sentence – the greeting is actually based on the sentence ‘Today is a good day’ (こんにち [today] は [topic particle] いい [good] ひ [day] です). So, to write こんにちは correctly, use the topic particle は instead of the character わ.

==== おはようございます (ohayou gozaimasu)

This greeting means ‘Good morning’ and is used in formal settings such as the workplace or with someone you don’t know well. The casual version is おはよう (ohayou), which can be used between family members and friends.

==== ありがとうございます (arigatou gozaimasu)

This phrase means ‘Thank you very much’ and is used in formal settings. The casual version is ありがとう (arigatou), which can be used between family members and friends.

==== どういたしまして (dou itashi mashite)

This phrase is similar to ‘You’re welcome’ in English. It is used to respond to expressions of gratitude in a polite and humble way.

==== すみません (sumimasen)

This phrase means ‘Excuse me’ or ‘I’m sorry’ depending on the situation, and can be used in formal settings.

NOTE: 口语中，「悪い」 经常用来代替正式的「すみません」，表示轻微的道歉或不好意思。注意这只用于熟人/朋友之间，对长辈或上司不能说「悪い」。例句——踩到别人：悪い！<-->すみません！；请求帮助：悪いけど、ちょっと聞（き）いて。<-->すみませんが、お聞きしてもよろしいですか；迟到了：遅れて悪い。<-->遅（おく）れてすみません。

[cols="1,1,2,2",opts="header"]
|===
| 简体否定	| 悪くない	| 悪くないね。　	| 不坏嘛 / 挺好的
| 敬体　　	| 悪いです	| すみません、悪いです。	| 不好意思（是我不好）
| 过去式　	| 悪かった	| 悪かったよ。　	| 是我的错 / 对不住了
| て形　　	| 悪くて　	| 悪くてごめん。	| 是我的错，对不起
|===


==== ごめんなさい (gomen nasai)

This phrase means ‘I’m sorry’ and is used when apologising to someone.

The phrases すみません (sumimasen) and ごめんなさい (gomen nasai) both mean ‘I’m sorry’ in Japanese, but they are not completely interchangeable.

ごめんなさい is a bit more informal, though still polite, and is often used in casual apologies, whereas すみません is more formal and professional.

Notably, すみません is used more widely than ごめんなさい, serving not only to apologise but also to get someone’s attention or to excuse oneself in minor situations, like when you’re trying to make your way through a crowd.

'''''

Remember, when greeting someone in Japanese, it is important to consider the level of formality required for the situation. In general, using polite language, with endings such as です (desu) and ござい（ます）(gozai[masu]), is appropriate in formal settings, while shorter and more casual versions are used among friends and family members.

Choose the most appropriate expression for each situation.
